In the aftermath, she threw herself into supporting the staff, giving statements to police, and designing new safety protocols, while privately trying to keep up her usual commitments to mentoring, salsa class, and church activities as if nothing had changed. Over the following year, she started quietly avoiding evening events in the neighborhood where the robbery took place, became more reluctant to drive alone at night, and found that her enjoyment of museum visits and jazz concerts was increasingly overshadowed by a sense of dread in crowded or unfamiliar spaces, which began to interfere with her work travel and community leadership roles. When she turned down a speaking invitation for a women’s leadership conference because it was scheduled for an evening in a downtown venue and realized she was arranging her life around not feeling safe, she discussed it with a close friend from her business network, who suggested she see a psychiatrist through her primary care clinic.\n- Chief Complaint: “Ever since that robbery, I keep organizing my whole life around avoiding anything that reminds me of it—certain neighborhoods, late meetings, even some community events.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I lie there for an hour or more with my mind replaying the day and that night at the store, and when I finally do fall asleep I wake up two or three times, heart racing, and feel like I haven’t really rested at all when the alarm goes off at six.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere—like when I’m locking up an office or I hear a loud male voice behind me—I’ll suddenly see myself back on that floor with the gun at my head, and for a few seconds it feels so real that my chest tightens and I can’t catch my breath.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I’ve started scheduling all my client visits for mornings or early afternoons, avoiding that whole part of town after dark, and I make excuses to skip evening church events or networking mixers if they’re in busy downtown areas, because just the thought of being in a store or parking garage at night makes my skin crawl.\n- S071 (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): I can remember lying on the floor and hearing the kid crying and the shouting, but there are chunks of that night—like what exactly the robber said to me or how long it lasted—that are just blank no matter how hard I try to piece it together.\n- S072 (Negative_Beliefs): Since the robbery, I catch myself thinking, ‘You were stupid to be there that late, you should’ve known better,’ and more generally that the world’s just gotten ugly and dangerous and you can’t really let your guard down anywhere anymore.\n- S075 (Social_Detachment): Even when I’m at church or at a family barbecue, I feel like I’m watching everybody from behind glass, nodding and smiling in the right places but not really feeling like I’m part of it or that anyone truly gets what’s going on with me.\n- S076 (Inability_Positive_Emotions): Things that used to light me up—like planning a big sales meeting, going to salsa class, or taking my granddaughter to the museum—now just feel flat, like I’m going through the motions because I’m supposed to enjoy them but nothing really reaches me.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I find myself snapping at my staff or my sister over little things, like someone being five minutes late or misplacing a file, and I hear the sharpness in my own voice but it’s like the anger is already out before I can pull it back.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Wherever I go now, I’m automatically checking who’s coming in and out, where the exits are, and what kind of people are hanging around, and I have a hard time relaxing at a restaurant or store because a part of me is always on watch.\n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): If someone comes up behind me at work or a car door slams nearby, I jump out of my skin—my heart just takes off, my whole body tenses, and it takes several minutes before I can shake off that jolt and focus again.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last year I’ve turned down promotions, evening speaking gigs, and most downtown events, and I’ve pulled back from mentoring and church activities so much that my coworkers and friends have started asking why I’m not showing up like I used to.\n- duration: These problems have been hanging over me pretty steadily since a few months after the robbery, so it’s been going on for well over a year now.\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can specifically answer based on the profile, set matched=true, list related\n In the aftermath, she threw herself into supporting the staff, giving statements to police, and designing new safety protocols, while privately trying to keep up her usual commitments to mentoring, salsa class, and church activities as if nothing had changed. When she turned down a speaking invitation for a women’s leadership conference because it was scheduled for an evening in a downtown venue and realized she was arranging her life around not feeling up—like planning a big sales meeting, going to salsa class, or taking my granddaughter to the museum—now just feel flat, like I’m going through the motions because I’m supposed to enjoy them but the sharpness in my own voice but it’s like the anger is already out before I can pull it back.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Wherever I go now, I’m automatically checking who’s coming in and out, where nothing really reaches me.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I find myself snapping at my staff or my sister over little things, like someone being five minutes late or misplacing a file, and I hear the sharpness in my own voice but it’s like the anger is already out before I can pull it back.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Wherever I go now, I’m automatically checking who’s coming in and out, where In the aftermath, she threw herself into supporting the staff, giving statements to police, and designing new safety protocols, while privately trying to keep up her usual commitments to mentoring, salsa class, and church activities as if nothing had changed. Over the following year, she started quietly avoiding evening events in the neighborhood where the robbery took place, became more reluctant to drive alone at night, and found that her enjoyment of museum visits and jazz concerts was increasingly overshadowed by a sense of dread in crowded or unfamiliar spaces, which began to interfere with her work travel and community leadership roles. When she turned down a speaking invitation for a women’s leadership conference because it was scheduled for an evening in a downtown venue and realized she was arranging her life around not feeling safe, she discussed it with a close friend from her business network, who suggested she see a psychiatrist through her primary care clinic.\n- Chief Complaint: “Ever since that robbery, I keep organizing my whole life around avoiding anything that reminds me of it—certain neighborhoods, late meetings, even some community events.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I lie there for an hour or more with my mind replaying the day and that night at the store, and when I finally do fall asleep I wake up two or three times, heart racing, and feel like I haven’t really rested at all when the alarm goes off at six.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere—like when I’m locking up an office or I hear a loud male voice behind me—I’ll suddenly see myself back on that floor with the gun at my head, and for a few seconds it feels so real that my chest tightens and I can’t catch my breath.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I’ve started scheduling all my client visits for mornings or early afternoons, avoiding that whole part of town after dark, and I make excuses to skip evening church events or networking mixers if they’re kid crying and the shouting, but there are chunks of that night—like what exactly the robber said to me or how long it lasted—that are just blank no matter how hard I try to piece it together.\n- S072 and you can’t really let your guard down anywhere anymore.\n- S075 (Social_Detachment): Even when I’m at church or at a family barbecue, I feel like I’m watching everybody from behind glass, nodding and smiling in the right places but not really feeling like I’m part of it or that anyone truly gets what’s going on with me.\n- S076 (Inability_Positive_Emotions): Things that used to light me up—like planning a big sales meeting, going to salsa class, or taking my granddaughter to the museum—now just feel flat, like I’m going through the motions because I’m supposed to enjoy them but nothing really reaches me.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I find myself snapping at my staff or my sister over little things, like someone being five minutes late or misplacing a file, and I hear the sharpness in my own voice but it’s like the anger is already out before I can pull it back.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Wherever I go now, I’m automatically checking who’s coming in and out, where the exits are, and what kind of people are hanging around, and I have a hard time relaxing at a restaurant or store because a part of me is always on watch.\n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): If someone comes up behind me at work or a car door slams nearby, I jump out of my skin—my heart just takes off, my whole body tenses, and it takes several minutes before I can shake off that jolt and focus again.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last year I’ve turned down promotions, evening speaking gigs, and most downtown events, and I’ve pulled back from What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Ever since that robbery, I keep organizing my whole life around avoiding anything that reminds me of it—certain neighborhoods, late meetings, even some community events. I realized I've been arranging my entire life around just trying to feel safe, and a close friend suggested I finally come in to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically happens when you're in a situation or place that reminds you of the robbery, how do you feel and what do you do?\n\nPatient: To avoid reminders, I only schedule client visits in the morning and completely stay away from that part of town after dark. If I am triggered, like when locking up an office, I suddenly see myself back on that floor with the gun at my head, my chest tightens, and I can't catch my breath. I also feel incredibly anxious in those moments, constantly scanning for exits and jumping out of my skin at any sudden noise.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience excessive and persistent worry about everyday things, not just situations related to the robbery, and find it difficult to control this worry?\n\nPatient: No, my worries are almost entirely focused on my safety and things that remind me of the robbery. I don't really experience uncontrollable worry about other normal, everyday matters. It's really just about trying to keep myself safe and avoiding those triggers.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much of your daily life or activities have been affected by these avoidance behaviors and flashbacks, would you say it's significantly impacted your social, work, or personal relationships?\n\nPatient: It has had a massive impact, and I feel like my whole world has shrunk. Over the past year, I've turned down promotions, evening speaking gigs, and pulled back from my mentoring and church activities. It's gotten to the point where my friends and coworkers have started asking why I'm not showing up like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any difficulty recalling specific details of the robbery, or if there are parts of that event that you can't remember at all?\n\nPatient: Yes, there are definitely blank spots from that night that I just can't recall. While I vividly remember lying on the floor and hearing the young employee crying, other parts—like what the robber actually said to me or how long it all lasted—are completely blank no matter how hard I try to piece it together.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel as though you're observing yourself from outside your body or as if you're in a dream during these flashbacks or when confronted with reminders of the robbery?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel like I'm outside of my body or in a dream at all. Instead, I feel very much trapped in my own body, with my chest tightening up, my heart racing, and feeling an intense, overwhelming fear."

In the aftermath, she threw herself into supporting the staff, giving statements to police, and designing new safety protocols, while privately trying to keep up her usual commitments to mentoring, salsa class, and church activities as if nothing had changed. Over the following year, she started quietly avoiding evening events in the neighborhood where the robbery took place, became more reluctant to drive alone at night, and found that her enjoyment of museum visits and jazz concerts was increasingly overshadowed by a sense of dread in crowded or unfamiliar spaces, which began to interfere with her work travel and community leadership roles. When she turned down a speaking invitation for a women’s leadership conference because it was scheduled for an evening in a downtown venue and realized she was arranging her life around not feeling safe, she discussed it with a close friend from her business network, who suggested she see a psychiatrist through her primary care clinic.\n- Chief Complaint: “Ever since that robbery, I keep organizing my whole life around avoiding anything that reminds me of it—certain neighborhoods, late meetings, even some community events.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I lie there for an hour or more with my mind replaying the day and that night at the store, and when I finally do fall asleep I wake up two or three times, heart racing, and feel like I haven’t really rested at all when the alarm goes off at six.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere—like when I’m locking up an office or I hear a loud male voice behind me—I’ll suddenly see myself back on that floor with the gun at my head, and for a few seconds it feels so real that my chest tightens and I can’t catch my breath.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I’ve started scheduling all my client visits for mornings or early afternoons, avoiding that whole part of town after dark, and I make excuses to skip evening church events or networking mixers if they’re in busy downtown areas, because just the thought of being in a store or parking garage at night makes my skin crawl.\n- S071 (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): I can remember lying on the floor and hearing the kid crying and the shouting, but there are chunks of that night—like what exactly the robber said to me or how long it lasted—that are just blank no matter how hard I try to piece it together.\n- S072 (Negative_Beliefs): Since the robbery, I catch myself thinking, ‘You were stupid to be there that late, you should’ve known better,’ and more generally that the world’s just gotten ugly and dangerous and you can’t really let your guard down anywhere anymore.\n- S075 (Social_Detachment): Even when I’m at church or at a family barbecue, I feel like I’m watching everybody from behind glass, nodding and smiling in the right places but not really feeling like I’m part of it or that anyone truly gets what’s going on with me.\n- S076 (Inability_Positive_Emotions): Things that used to light me up—like planning a big sales meeting, going to salsa class, or taking my granddaughter to the museum—now just feel flat, like I’m going through the motions because I’m supposed to enjoy them but nothing really reaches me.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I find myself snapping at my staff or my sister over little things, like someone being five minutes late or misplacing a file, and I hear the sharpness in my own voice but it’s like the anger is already out before I can pull it back.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Wherever I go now, I’m automatically checking who’s coming in and out, where the exits are, and what kind of people are hanging around, and I have a hard time relaxing at a restaurant or store because a part of me is always on watch.\n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): If someone comes up behind me at work or a car door slams nearby, I jump out of my skin—my heart just takes off, my whole body tenses, and it takes several minutes before I can shake off that jolt and focus again.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last year I’ve turned down promotions, evening speaking gigs, and most downtown events, and I’ve pulled back from mentoring and church activities so much that my coworkers and friends have started asking why I’m not showing up like I used to.\n- duration: These problems have been hanging over me pretty steadily Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Ever since that robbery, I keep organizing my whole life around avoiding anything that reminds me of it—certain neighborhoods, late meetings, even some community events. I realized I've been arranging my entire life around just trying to feel safe, and a close friend suggested I finally come in to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically happens when you're in a situation or place that reminds you of the robbery, how do you feel and what do you do?\n\nPatient: To avoid reminders, I only schedule client visits in the morning and completely stay away from that part of town after dark. If I am triggered, like when locking up an office, I suddenly see myself back on that floor with the gun at my head, my chest tightens, and I can't catch my breath. I also feel incredibly anxious in those moments, constantly scanning for exits and jumping out of my skin at any sudden noise.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience excessive and persistent worry about everyday things, not just situations related to the robbery, and find it difficult to control this worry?\n\nPatient: No, my worries are almost entirely focused on my safety and things that remind me of the robbery. I don't really experience uncontrollable worry about other normal, everyday matters. It's really just about trying to keep myself safe and avoiding those triggers.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much of your daily life or activities have been affected by these avoidance behaviors and flashbacks, would you say it's significantly impacted your social, work, or personal relationships?\n\nPatient: It has had a massive impact, and I feel like my whole world has shrunk. It's gotten to the point where my friends and coworkers have started asking why I'm not showing up like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any difficulty recalling specific details of the robbery, or if there are parts of that event that you can't remember at all?\n\nPatient: Yes, there are definitely blank spots from that night that I just can't recall. While I vividly remember lying on the floor and hearing the young employee crying, other parts—like what the robber actually said to me or how long it all lasted—are completely blank no matter how hard I try to piece it together.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel as though you're observing yourself from outside your body or as if you're in a dream during these flashbacks or when confronted with reminders of the robbery?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel like I'm outside of my body or in a dream at all. Instead, I feel very much trapped in my own body, with my chest tightening up, my heart racing, and feeling an intense, overwhelming fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any feelings of detachment from others or the world around you, as if people or things seem unreal or distant, especially when not having a flashback?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely feel that way, like I'm watching everyone from behind glass even when I'm at church or a family barbecue. I nod and smile in the right places, but I feel completely disconnected and like I'm not really part of it. It just feels like nobody truly understands what I'm going through anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you also experience significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other areas of functioning due to these feelings of detachment or avoidance behaviors?"

In the aftermath, she threw herself into supporting the staff, giving statements to police, and designing new safety protocols, while privately trying to keep up her usual commitments to mentoring, salsa class, and church activities as if nothing had changed. Over the following year, she started quietly avoiding evening events in the neighborhood where the robbery took place, became more reluctant to drive alone at night, and found that her enjoyment of museum visits and jazz concerts was increasingly overshadowed by a sense of dread in crowded or unfamiliar spaces, which began to interfere with her work travel and community leadership roles. When she turned down a speaking invitation for a women’s leadership conference because it was scheduled for an evening in a downtown venue and realized she was arranging her life around not feeling safe, she discussed it with a close friend from her business network, who suggested she see a psychiatrist through her primary care clinic.\n- Chief Complaint: “Ever since that robbery, I keep organizing my whole life around avoiding anything that reminds me of it—certain neighborhoods, late meetings, even some community events.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I lie there for an hour or more with my mind replaying the day and that night at the store, and when I finally do fall asleep I wake up two or three times, heart racing, and feel like I haven’t really rested at all when the alarm goes off at six.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere—like when I’m locking up an office or I hear a loud male voice behind me—I’ll suddenly see myself back on that floor with the gun at my head, and for a few seconds it feels so real that my chest tightens and I can’t catch my breath.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I’ve started scheduling all my client visits for mornings or early afternoons, avoiding that whole part of town after dark, and I make excuses to skip evening church events or networking mixers if they’re in busy downtown areas, because just the thought of being in a store or parking garage at night makes my skin crawl.\n- S071 (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): I can remember lying on the floor and hearing the kid crying and the shouting, but there are chunks of that night—like what exactly the robber said to me or how long it lasted—that are just blank no matter how hard I try to piece it together.\n- S072 (Negative_Beliefs): Since the robbery, I catch myself thinking, ‘You were stupid to be there that late, you should’ve known better,’ and more generally that the world’s just gotten ugly and dangerous and you can’t really let your guard down anywhere anymore.\n- S075 (Social_Detachment): Even when I’m at church or at a family barbecue, I feel like I’m watching everybody from behind glass, nodding and smiling in the right places but not really feeling like I’m part of it or that anyone truly gets what’s going on with me.\n- S076 (Inability_Positive_Emotions): Things that used to light me up—like planning a big sales meeting, going to salsa class, or taking my granddaughter to the museum—now just feel flat, like I’m going through the motions because I’m supposed to enjoy them but nothing really reaches me.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I find myself snapping at my staff or my sister over little things, like someone being five minutes late or misplacing a file, and I hear the sharpness in my own voice but it’s like the anger is already out before I can pull it back.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Wherever I go now, I’m automatically checking who’s coming in and out, where the exits are, and what kind of people are hanging around, and I have a hard time relaxing at a restaurant or store because a part of me is always on watch.\n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): If someone comes up behind me at work or a car door slams nearby, I jump out of my skin—my heart just takes off, my whole body tenses, and it takes several minutes before I can shake off that jolt and focus again.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last year I’ve turned down promotions, evening speaking gigs, and most downtown events, and I’ve pulled back from mentoring and church activities so much that my coworkers and friends have started asking why I’m not showing up like I used to.\n- duration: These problems have been hanging over me pretty steadily since a few months after the robbery, so it’s been going on for well over a year now.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “Ever since that robbery, I keep organizing my whole life around avoiding anything that reminds me of it—certain neighborhoods, late meetings, even some community events.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: S072 (Negative_Beliefs)\nAnswer strategy: Confirm that you have been struggling with these thoughts. Share that you often blame yourself, thinking you were stupid to be at the store so late and should have known better. Explain that you also feel the world has become an ugly and dangerous place, and you feel like you can never truly let your guard down anymore."

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Ever since that robbery, I keep organizing my whole life around avoiding anything that reminds me of it—certain neighborhoods, late meetings, even some community events. I realized I've been arranging my entire life around just trying to feel safe, and a close friend suggested I finally come in to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically happens when you're in a situation or place that reminds you of the robbery, how do you feel and what do you do?\n\nPatient: To avoid reminders, I only schedule client visits in the morning and completely stay away from that part of town after dark. If I am triggered, like when locking up an office, I suddenly see myself back on that floor with the gun at my head, my chest tightens, and I can't catch my breath. I also feel incredibly anxious in those moments, constantly scanning for exits and jumping out of my skin at any sudden noise.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience excessive and persistent worry about everyday things, not just situations related to the robbery, and find it difficult to control this worry?\n\nPatient: No, my worries are almost entirely focused on my safety and things that remind me of the robbery. I don't really experience uncontrollable worry about other normal, everyday matters. It's really just about trying to keep myself safe and avoiding those triggers.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much of your daily life or activities have been affected by these avoidance behaviors and flashbacks, would you say it's significantly impacted your social, work, or personal relationships?\n\nPatient: It has had a massive impact, and I feel like my whole world has shrunk. Over the past year, I've turned down promotions, evening speaking gigs, and pulled back from my mentoring and church activities. It's gotten to the point where my friends and coworkers have started asking why I'm not showing up like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any difficulty recalling specific details of the robbery, or if there are parts of that event that you can't remember at all?\n\nPatient: Yes, there are definitely blank spots from that night that I just can't recall. While I vividly remember lying on the floor and hearing the young employee crying, other parts—like what the robber actually said to me or how long it all lasted—are completely blank no matter how hard I try to piece it together.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel as though you're observing yourself from outside your body or as if you're in a dream during these flashbacks or when confronted with reminders of the robbery?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel like I'm outside of my body or in a dream at all. Instead, I feel very much trapped in my own body, with my chest tightening up, my heart racing, and feeling an intense, overwhelming fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any feelings of detachment from others or the world around you, as if people or things seem unreal or distant, especially when not having a flashback?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely feel that way, like I'm watching everyone from behind glass even when I'm at church or a family barbecue. I nod and smile in the right places, but I feel completely disconnected and like I'm not really part of it. It just feels like nobody truly understands what I'm going through anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you also experience significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other areas of functioning due to these feelings of detachment or avoidance behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has severely impacted my life across the board. I've turned down promotions, evening speaking engagements, and downtown events, and I've pulled back from mentoring and church activities. It's gotten to the point where my friends and coworkers are constantly asking why I'm not showing up like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about yourself, others, or the world, such as feeling guilty for surviving the robbery or believing that the world is completely unsafe?"

nothing really reaches me.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I find myself snapping at my staff or my sister over little things, like someone being five minutes late or misplacing a file, and I hear the sharpness in my own voice but it’s like the anger is already out before I can pull it back.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Wherever I go now, I’m automatically checking who’s coming in and out, where the exits are, and what kind of people are hanging around, and I have a hard time relaxing at a restaurant or store because a part of me is always on watch.\n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): If someone comes up behind me at work or a car door slams nearby, I jump out of my skin—my heart just takes off, my whole body tenses, and it takes several minutes before I can shake off that jolt and
